Unset, the row decides; with DLSS, DLAA or FSR drawing from the tone mapped picture (Upscale before bloom off) the game's SMAA is left out (none: they want jittered frames no other anti-aliasing has been over), and before the bloom it runs as the game's; the switch goes over both, for an A/B (`RX_VK_AA=0`: the game's SMAA under the upscaler). The game's SMAA is three compute programs known by their keys (game build `e0f3ccfa`): with another choice in effect its composition is replaced and its edge and weight passes are left out, and `RX_VK_AA_KEEP=1` keeps those two running. The log says `vk: aa:` when a composition is first replaced, why one was left to the game, and when none was found for a minute |
| `RX_VK_THREADED=0`, `=1`, `=auto` | (Triforce; **off by default**, the Debug tab's Threaded rendering row, Off, On or Auto, which this goes over for the run) The renderer's threads as one design (`gpu/vk/threaded_rendering.h`), each piece one Triforce has, driven from here: the replay on its thread (`RX_VK_ASYNC=1`), the recorder thread (`RX_VK_RECORDER=thread`) handed a part at a time (`RX_VK_RECORDER_PARTS`), a submission's parts ending where the game ends its render pass (`RX_VK_PART_MIN`, `RX_VK_PART_FIRST`, `RX_VK_PART_MAX`), the present thread (`RX_VK_PRESENT_THREAD`) asking for the next image right after each present (`RX_VK_ACQUIRE_AHEAD`). Each of those switches, when set, still goes over it. Auto: on from 4 physical cores in the process's affinity; with no buffer-pool mirrors the recorder thread alone from 6; off with `RX_VK_CAPTURE=0`, `RX_VK_LOW_SPEC`, `RX_VK_DUMP` or `RX_VK_BENCH`. `=0` (or the row Off): everything as before. The log's `vk: Threaded rendering:` line says what this run took. The lab's A/B is `RX_VK_THREADED=0` against `=1` |
| `RX_VK_ASYNC=1` | The replay on a thread of its own instead of the game's submitting thread (off by default). The game still presents only once the GPU has run its frame, so nothing is released early. (Triforce, `gpu/vk/async_rules.h`; not yet run in the game) With it on, each with a switch of its own to put it back: a signal of the CPU's takes its capture on the game's thread only when a submission handed over before it may still wait for that event, which the host tells at the submit (`RX_VK_ASYNC_EVENT_NOTES=0`: at every signal while the thread has work, as before; the `vk: the replay thread: ... signals of the CPU's` line counts both); the submit's capture shares its copies with the copy helpers and has them all made before the hand-over (`RX_VK_ASYNC_CAPTURE_BEHIND=0`: every copy on the game's thread); the game's submit keeps its virtual core for the hand-over (`HOS_ASYNC_KEEP_CORE=0`: given up and taken again); the game's present poll waits for the replay thread asleep and looks at its event between yields only once the GPU has the frame (`HOS_PRESENT_POLL=0`: the game's own sleeps); a present runs the jobs behind it until the frame's stopped work has gone on and then makes go only what was already stopped at the present before (`RX_VK_PRESENT_DRAIN=1`: every present forces every stopped queue past its wait, as before; `=0`: none does; `RX_VK_PRESENT_WAIT_MS`, 8: how long it runs the jobs behind); and the thread looks for its next job for 100 µs before it sleeps (`RX_VK_ASYNC_SPIN_US=<0..1000>`). Still open, and why it stays off: after a ten-area tour the player's character is drawn black for one frame, 0 to 3 times a minute at the Market (`gpu/vk/async_rules.h` has what the lab ruled out). Two diagnostics for it: `RX_VK_LOG_READBACK_ZERO=1` logs each readback of GPU results that is all 0 where it was not at its readback before, and `RX_VK_REGION_TRAP=1` (with `RX_VK_PASS_TRACE=1` and the frame log) logs the passes of the last frames and writes their render conditions to `region_cond_<n>.txt` when the picture's bottom middle goes dark for a frame, eight times a run. `RX_VK_ASYNC_FORCED=1`: the thread as it was before 11-14, every catch-up forcing |
| `RX_VK_OCC_QUERY=0`, `RX_VK_OCC_QUERY_SKIP=<addr>[,...]`, `RX_VK_OCC_COND=0`, `RX_VK_OCC_LOG_COND=1`, `RX_VK_OCC_GATE=0` | (on by default since 11-17: +2.7 to +4.6% fps in every scene measured, 10-12% fewer draws, no flicker added; `RX_VK_OCC_QUERY=0` turns it off) The game's samples-passed reports (which objects show; a count of 0 is not drawn) get their real counts: the replay still writes each report as it passes it with 4096 (`RX_VK_OCC_FIXED`, everything visible), and the draws from its reset are counted by Vulkan occlusion queries (precise where the card has them); once the GPU is done with the recording that holds them the count is written into the report's record over the 4096, as NX2navi writes it at completion. The done recordings are found with one look at the recordings' timeline (nothing waits) as a game's fence wait ends (the game reads its counts after it), at each recording's begin and each present, at the latest when the recording's slot comes round. The game's report records rotate (a ring: an address is another object's at its next report), so a count is written only while its report is still the newest at its address, and only when it is whole (every query available, no draw left out, reset and report in one recording, read back within 8 presents); otherwise the record keeps its 4096. The game also predicates draws and clears on these words (render conditions), decided as NX2navi decides them: a word whose newest report is a samples-passed one whose count is still to come is unknown and the condition renders; a word whose count was written (or for which none comes) is read from memory as before, where a written 0 skips the draws and clears it gates. A count goes in only over the value the replay left in the word (a compare-and-write: never over the game's CPU re-arming the record), and a record rewritten in the stream after its report (a small buffer clear, a report of another counter type) gets no count. A parallel replay's segments signal the recordings' timeline and are sealed as they are submitted, so their counts are written too. The count word is written as the replay writes a report (`guest_cpu_write`, and the word kept out of the mirrors' write-backs). With hostQueryReset (Vulkan 1.2) a recording's queries are reset on the CPU once read, only those it used. `RX_VK_OCC_QUERY_SKIP`: report addresses that keep the fixed answer (the log's `occlusion query sites` lines list the first 64 addresses with their counts). The log's `occlusion queries` line every 600 presents: the reports, how many were counted whole and why not, the counts written at completion (and of them 0), those not written and why (lost, superseded by a later report at the address, late, a render condition's), the mean latency in presents and how many were written after the next present, where the done recordings were found, and the render conditions on samples-passed words (rendered because a count was pending, rendered or skipped from a written count, from memory with no count to come, as before in the knob's off phases) with the draws and clears they skipped. `RX_VK_OCC_COND=0`: a condition on a samples-passed word always renders (the counts are still written), to tell the counts from the skips. `RX_VK_OCC_LOG_COND=1`: one such condition in 499 logged (300 lines), with both words, their sources (pending, written, memory) and the present. (11-22) `RX_VK_OCC_GATE=0` turns off (on by default since its lab A/B: no fps lost at the Market): a count over a draw that a written count's condition left out (between the reset and its report) is the skip's 0, not the object's, and a condition on it renders; the count is still written for the game's CPU. Why: the game's CPU reads a count after its frame wait and draws a hidden object's proxy instead of the object, but most counts land after it has read the 4096, so it goes on drawing the object under the condition, the condition reads the skip's 0 and the object stays hidden (tickets 11-21: items and trees in the Forest Temple, black interiors; `RX_VK_OCC_QUERY=0` showed them). The line's second half counts these counts (counted whole, written, of them 0), the conditions that read one and those the switch rendered, on or off. Cost with it on: a hidden object under such a condition is drawn every other frame instead of never. Lab: the knob `occq` in `RX_VK_LAB_AB` runs the queries the whole run and writes the counts of the reports made in its phases (`gpu/vk/occ_answer.h`, `vk_replay.cpp` "occlusion queries") |
| `RX_VK_OCC_DEPTH_ONLY=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's GPUWORK-01, after Source A's; off unless set) Only the occlusion windows whose draws write no colour are counted. The game's occlusion tests draw depth only, but a query here opens at a window's first draw in the pass and stays open to the report or the pass's end, so it can enclose draws that write colour, and a query's end waits for the work it encloses. With the switch, a window's first draw that writes a colour target gives its count up: the open query ends before that draw, no query begins again until the next reset, and the report is incomplete, so its record keeps the 4096 placeholder and what it gates is drawn. Fewer objects are culled with it (Source A: 4% fewer for 0.25 ms of GPU time), never one missing; whether that pays here is for a measurement to say, which is why it is off. The lab's knob `occdepth` (`RX_VK_LAB_AB`) is the switch in its phases. Counted either way, in a third `vk: occlusion queries` line every 600 presents: the counting windows that met a colour draw, the colour draws recorded under an open query, the counts given up and the reports kept 4096 for it (`gpu/vk/occ_answer.h` `Counter::colour_draw`; `gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `occ_draw_colour`; `gpu/vk/vk_draw.cpp` `draw`) |
| `RX_VK_OCC_GATED_VISIBLE=0` | (Triforce, 11-24; on unless set to 0) A samples-passed count of 0 over a draw that a written count's render condition left out is not written: its record keeps 4096, visible. The queries counted nothing for the draw that did not run, so that 0 is not known to be the object's; written, the game's CPU read it and stopped drawing an object that may be in view until its proxy's count brought it back (an object that comes and goes). A gated count above 0 and a 0 that is not gated are written as before. `=0`: the gated 0 is written, as before. The log's occlusion line (4) counts both. |
| `RX_VK_OCC_LEAN=3` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's DESC-82, after Source B's: its first step, the census; off unless set) The game's occlusion proxies write no colour, yet each runs its whole fragment shader and fills its fragment descriptors. With `3`, each draw an open occlusion query counts that writes no colour is classed (`gpu/vk/occ_lean.h`): lean, or the first refusal of a pipeline without the fragment stage (no fragment stage, depth out, sample mask, demote, stores, image writes, atomics, global memory, alpha to coverage, alpha test), and its fragment descriptors added to its class; every 600 presents `vk: occlusion proxies (RX_VK_OCC_LEAN=3` gives each class's draws a frame and descriptors a draw. Nothing else changes: no pipeline, key or descriptor. The entry's lean pipelines (`1`) and their check (`2`) are built only if the census finds more than one fragment descriptor a proxy to save; they are not built, and read as off. `tests/test_occ_lean.py` |
| `RX_VK_GATE_WAIT_MS=<1..100>` | (Triforce, off unless set, after NX2navi's `gate_wait_ms`, whose default is 10; not yet run in the game) A submission whose first command buffer begins (after state only) with a wait for an event the game signals from the CPU, unmet in the replay's order, waits at its submit for that signal, at most this long, before its capture and its replay (`gpu/vk/gate_wait.h`, `gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `gate_wait_signal`): the capture then holds what the game wrote up to its signal, and the submission is replayed in the submit call on the submitting thread, instead of stopping at the wait and going on from the order thread (`sync_watch`) with a second capture once the signal came. The wait holds no lock and looks at the event's word in the game's memory. One that runs out goes as before; after 4 that ran out, once they are more than a tenth, it is off for the run (logged). A `vk: RX_VK_GATE_WAIT_MS` line every 600 waits gives their mean and how many ran out; the order line's `captures at a CPU event's wait` and `made to go by presents` say how often the old way was taken |
| `RX_VK_WAIT_HOLD_PAUSE_US=<us>` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's THREAD-11, after Source B's; off unless set, not measured here) A wait of the game's for a fence whose work is stopped at an event's wait holds up to `RX_VK_WAIT_HOLD_US` (3 ms) for another queue to let it on before it forces it, looking again and again: a flush, the timeline's value, the order lock to ask whether the stopped work is still needed, and a yield of the processor. With `<us>` (2 is Source B's; 1000 at most) a gap of PAUSEs that long stands between two looks instead of the yield, and the order lock is taken only when one of its sections has left since the waiter last asked, which is the only time its answer can change; the fence's own value is still read at every look, and the bound and the forced drain stay. Source B measured 59.2 to 59.9 fps and a 1% low of 40.0 to 42.1 with such a gap on a loop that runs far more often than this one (about 15 holds a second here). `0` or not set: as before. Either way the `vk: queues ordered by their events` report is followed by a `vk: fence waits held for stopped work` line: the holds, their looks, the time held, the takes of the order lock and how the holds ended (`gpu/vk/wait_hold.h`, `gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `wait`) |
| `RX_VK_WAIT_HOLD_PAUSE_US=<us>` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's THREAD-11, after Source B's; off unless set, not measured here) A wait of the game's for a fence whose work is stopped at an event's wait holds up to `RX_VK_WAIT_HOLD_US` (3 ms) for another queue to let it on before it forces it, looking again and again: a flush, the timeline's value, the order lock to ask whether the stopped work is still needed, and a yield of the processor. With `<us>` (2 is Source B's; 1000 at most) a gap of PAUSEs that long stands between two looks instead of the yield, and the order lock is taken only when one of its sections has left since the waiter last asked, which is the only time its answer can change, or while a signal of the CPU's (`nvnEventSignal` without the replay thread) is queued and no section has acted on it yet; the fence's own value is still read at every look, and the bound and the forced drain stay. Source B measured 59.2 to 59.9 fps and a 1% low of 40.0 to 42.1 with such a gap on a loop that runs far more often than this one (about 15 holds a second here). `0` or not set: as before. Either way the `vk: queues ordered by their events` report is followed by a `vk: fence waits held for stopped work` line: the holds, their looks, the time held, the takes of the order lock and how the holds ended (`gpu/vk/wait_hold.h`, `gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `wait`) |
| `RX_VK_INDEX_REBASE=0` | (Triforce, 11-21, the performance audit's DESC-12) A direct indexed draw binds its index buffer at offset 0 and gives its offset as the draw's first index, so draws at different offsets of one buffer bind it once: bound at each draw's own offset, nearly every indexed draw called `vkCmdBindIndexBuffer`. Only where the offset is a whole number of indices; indirect indexed draws are as before. `RX_VK_PROF_FINE=1` counts the index binds looked up and those made. `0`: every draw binds at its own offset (`gpu/vk/index_rebase.h`, `vk_draw.cpp` `draw`, `bind_index`) |
| `RX_VK_RECORDER_OWN=0` | (Triforce, 11-21, the performance audit's REPLAY-04) With the recorder thread, a recording slot's descriptor pools are reset, its extra pools destroyed and its grown streams given back in `draw_begin_recording`, after the recording's first packets, as before: each is a boundary the replay waits at until the recorder has run what came before. The default makes that reset in `begin_recording` right after the slot's command pools are reset, where the recorder has nothing held. The 120-present line `a frame's recorder thread` counts the replay's waits (`gpu/vk/vk_draw.cpp` `draw_slot_reset`, `vk_replay.cpp` `begin_recording`) |
| `RX_VK_DESC_POOL_GROW=0`, `RX_VK_DESC_POOL_DIV=<1..32>`, `RX_VK_STREAM_MB=<MB>` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's DESC-33, after Source A's; `gpu/vk/desc_pool_grow.h`) Each recording slot (48 at most) keeps a descriptor pool for the programs over the push limit, and the driver may keep host memory for all of it 48 times. A slot's pool is made at half size (4096 sets, 16384 uniform buffers and sampled images, and so on) and made whole (8192 sets) at the slot's first reset after a recording ran out of it; a recording that runs out gets a whole extra pool on the spot, as it always did. `RX_VK_DESC_POOL_GROW=0`: whole pools from the start, as before. `RX_VK_DESC_POOL_DIV=<n>` divides both sizes by n. `RX_VK_STREAM_MB=<n>` is the size of each slot's first upload stream (2 by default; a recording that needs more is moved to a bigger one, kept for the slot's next 120 recordings; 24 with `RX_VK_STREAM_GROW_OFF=1`, which skips draws instead). Every 3000 recordings the log's two "the recording streams" lines say the most one recording held of its stream, how many slots are still at half, how many were made whole, the extra pools made, and the most sets and descriptors of each type one recording took: what a smaller default is judged by (the stream's stays 2 MB until those numbers are read in play) (`gpu/vk/vk_draw.cpp` `make_pool`, `slot_reset_locked`, `draw_begin_recording`) |
| `RX_VK_CAP_SPARE_FIT=0`, `RX_VK_CAP_STEP_MB=<n>` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's MEM-33; the first on by default, the second off unless set; not yet run in the game) `RX_VK_CAP_SPARE_FIT=0`: a capture with no spare buffer makes its own with the spares' lock held, takes any spare that fits, and a spare made ahead is made once, as before. Without it (`gpu/vk/cap_spare.h`, `gpu/vk/vk_memory.cpp` `cap_buf_get`) the buffer is made with the lock let go, so the replay's take does not wait behind a submitting thread's allocation to give a buffer back; where spares are not capped at 32 MB a capture leaves alone a spare of 32 MB or more that is over twice its size class, for the load that wants it; the spare a capture of a class of 32 MB or more takes is made again on a worker; a spare is made ahead only if it fits what spares may hold together with the ones held and being made, and a buffer given back counts the ones being made; when the allocation fails, any spare that fits, then the exact size. `RX_VK_CAP_STEP_MB=<n>`: where spares are capped at 32 MB (a card of 6.5 GB or less), a capture over 16 and up to 32 MB gets a buffer in steps of n MB, not the whole 32 MB (Source A: 4; its worst frames were worse in two pairs, so off here). The 600-take line counts the buffers made on a submitting thread and their ms |
| `RX_VK_MIRROR_PIECE_IDLE=<submissions>` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's MEM-26, its first step only; off unless set; not yet run in the game) A mirror in pieces keeps a piece's memory until the whole mirror goes, also where no game pool is left in its import. Giving such pieces back is NOT ported (the unbind and the free must wait for the GPU and for the bind timeline). What it would find is counted (`gpu/vk/piece_idle.h`): every 1200 submissions, after the `mirrors in pieces` line, the MB of pieces no game pool covers; with this switch each binding given a mirror in pieces also notes the submission in its pieces, and the line says how many of those MB no binding reached for that many submissions (600 is the wait the release would use). Under about 100 MB at the end of a tour on a 6 GB card (`RX_VK_TEST_CARD_MB=6144`) the release is not worth porting |
| `RX_VK_RANGE_WALK=0` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's MEM-42; on by default; not yet run in the game) The capture and collection loops (`gpu/vk/vk_memory.cpp` `memory_collect_written`, `capture_make`'s staging and behind loops, `take_capture`'s loops over the texture storage's ranges) ask of every page whether it lies in a sorted range list with a binary search, as before. Without it each loop has a cursor that only moves forward (`gpu/vk/range_walk.h`), one pass over the ranges for the loop; an address below the last one asked searches again, so any order gives the search's answers (`tests/test_range_walk.py` compares them). The `RX_VK_LAB_AB` knob `norangewalk` searches in its phases |
| `RX_VK_TABLE_STREAM=0` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's SYNC-04; on by default; not yet run in the game) The guest page table's changes of a recording (the table the shaders' pointers go through, `gpu/vk/vk_memory.cpp` `memory_table_flush`) are flushed as before: a (page, value) pair for every page changed, all of them sorted, the last of each page kept: 16 bytes a page and a sort on the recording thread, a few ms when a pool of hundreds of MB is imported ahead or merged. Without it the changes are streamed (`gpu/vk/table_stream.h`): runs in page order and apart are appended one after the other, any other set is swept by page with the newest change owning each page, through one reused batch of 8,192 entries. The transfers are the same ones in the same order (`tests/test_page_table_stream.py` compares the two, entry for entry). A flush over 1 ms is logged with its pages, changes and transfers (the first 16) |
| `RX_VK_CAPTURE_BEHIND=0` | (Triforce, on by default; `=0` as before) The submit-time capture (`gpu/vk/vk_memory.cpp` `capture_make`) takes the write marks on the game's submitting thread and copies there only the pages read at once (the late pages and the texture storage); the rest of its copies, in parts of 32 pages, go to the capture's copy helpers while that thread replays the submission (`gpu/vk/capture_behind.h`). A read of a page as submitted copies its part itself when no helper has taken it yet, or waits for the helper; every part is done and flushed before a recording is submitted and before the thread goes back to the game. Only with the replay on the submitting thread (not `RX_VK_ASYNC=1`), with copy helpers (`RX_VK_CAPTURE_THREADS`, not `RX_VK_LOW_SPEC`), at least 128 pages behind and at most 512 ahead (a scene's load: copied whole as before). At the Market the old copy was ~0.25 ms of each submit's ~0.35 ms capture, six submits a frame. The 120-present line `a frame's captures at the game's submits` gives their time on the game's threads (the scan, the copies made there, and the copies behind made or waited for by the replay), the MB copied and behind, and who copied the parts; the stall line names the captures when they took 1 ms or more |
| `RX_VK_CAP_FREE_ASYNC=0` | (Triforce, on by default; `=0` as before) A retired capture buffer the spares cannot keep (past 12 buffers or 768 MB, 32 MB on a card of 6.5 GB or less) is destroyed on a thread of its own (`gpu/vk/vk_memory.cpp` `cap_buf_free`, `destroy_buffer_later`), not in the capture's take on the replay's thread with its locks held: freeing a 64-256 MB host-cached buffer makes the driver unmap and release every page, the `retires` part of a `vk: slow capture take` line. Only buffers the GPU is done with get there; the memory comes back a moment later. The 600-take line counts the buffers and MB handed over. With no thread to be had they are destroyed where they were retired |
| `RX_VK_PARALLEL=<0..8>`, `RX_VK_PARALLEL_AUTO=<2..8>` | The parallel replay: a frame's large command buffers recorded on that many threads, always, or wherever short trials as the game runs find it faster than the replay thread alone; `RX_VK_PARALLEL=0`: never. The Debug tab's Replay helper threads row is the second without an environment to set (`replay_helper_threads=` 1 to 7 for 2 to 8 threads); either switch goes over the row. The row's Automatic (8), which it is unless the options file says otherwise, is the count `gpu/vk/replay_threads.h` gives the PC: none with four cores or fewer or on AMD's driver for RDNA 1 and 2, 2 threads with five to seven cores, 3 with eight or more. The log says at the start what was chosen (`replay helper threads:`) and at each trial which way is in use (`parallel replay, trial`). Between trials the tuner's rule (`gpu/vk/replay_threads.h` `rule`) judges every 120 frames on the replay thread alone: where the replay is plainly the frame's limit (70% of the frame or more) and the helper threads would take at least 1 ms and a tenth of the frame off it, never counting below the GPU's time (the game waits for each frame's GPU work), they are turned on without a trial; where they cannot pay (no command buffer large enough to cut, the GPU's time about as long as the replay's, or the replay under 60% of the frame: the game's own threads are its limit) no trial is made while that holds; trials decide the rest, and a trial that takes the helpers back off holds the rule's "on" for 3,600 presents. The log says so (`parallel replay, the rule`) when its verdict changes. `RX_VK_PARALLEL_RULE=0`: trials only, as before. With DLSS or FSR on, for upscaling or frame generation, the dry pass tells their hooks in the draws in stream order, and each command buffer from the first draw or dispatch that takes what they noted (the upscale, or the engine's point before the bloom) on is recorded in order after the segments before it (`gpu/vk/stream_order.h`); `RX_VK_PARALLEL_TEMPORAL=0`: the replay thread records alone then, as before. The frame statistics' `a frame's parallel replay:` line says in how many frames the threads recorded, the command buffers in segments and alone (and why), and the time in them; the next one (`its parts`) where that time went: the setup before the threads begin, how soon each command buffer's first segment reached the GPU, the helper threads' wake-up, each command buffer's longest segment, the replay thread's wait, the submits, the recording lock's waits, and the segments' GPU time (the `GPU time` line counts the segments too; it left them out). After each trial's line a `trial N's parts` line gives its three parts' frame, replay thread's time, GPU time, the rest of the frame, full barriers and draws, and the helper threads' parts. That header lists what an audit of the parallel replay found and fixed, and what is left. `RX_VK_NO_DRY_COPIES=1`: the dry pass does not mark what copies and texture clears write, as before. The replay thread records the next segment to submit itself when no helper thread has begun it (it only waited before, on the core kept for it); `RX_VK_PARALLEL_OWN=0`: it waits for the helper threads, as before. `RX_VK_PARALLEL_SEG=<32..>`: the draws and dispatches a segment is cut for (160); `RX_VK_PARALLEL_SEGS=<2..12>`: the most segments a command buffer is cut into (6), without which a smaller `RX_VK_PARALLEL_SEG` cuts no finer once a command buffer has six segments' worth |
| `RX_VK_PARALLEL_BACKOFF=0` | The tuner's trials as before: one every 1,800 presents after one that kept the way, each with 104 frames the other way. With the back-off (the default, `gpu/vk/replay_threads.h` `Tuner`) each trial that keeps the way, the other way longer than both of its own parts, puts the next one off twice as long (3,600, 7,200, then 14,400 presents at most; a margin of 10% or more counts twice), one too close to tell keeps the old interval (the first such one after a change of scene or way: 900 presents), and once the way has held so the other way is tried for 64 frames, not 104; a short trial that finds the other way shorter changes nothing itself, a full trial follows at once. The next trial comes early (900 presents after the last one at the soonest, never while the rule says no trial) when the scene clearly changes: two windows of 120 frames in a row whose draws moved by a quarter (200 at least) or whose replay or GPU time moved by 30% (1 ms at least) from the first window after the way was set, or a trial whose first and last parts did. In the lab's steady Market a trial came every ~2,090 presents, each ~100 frames 1.9 ms longer; the trial lines now end with when the next may come, and `parallel replay, the scene changed` says when one was brought forward |
| `RX_VK_TUNER_SETTLED=0` | The tuner's rule judges every window and a trial the scene changed under decides, as before. With settled scenes only (the default, `gpu/vk/replay_threads.h` `Tuner`, after Kaefergeneral's #dev-fixes post: on his 5900X and RTX 3090 the rule turned the helper threads on in an area's load and the first trial after the arrival kept them, and Automatic was 6 to 10% slower than Off) the rule judges a window of 120 frames only if the one before it describes the same scene (its draws within a quarter, 200 at least, its replay and GPU times within 30%, 1 ms at least: the back-off's test), so a load's window and the first after it get no verdict and the rule's "on" comes a window after a scene settles; a trial whose first and last parts moved so changes nothing, either way, and the next comes 120 presents after a window like the one before it, not 900 or more later. The trial's line says so (`the scene changed under it`), and `parallel replay, the scene settled` when the next is set. Between trials the scene is calm while two windows in a row of the way in use describe one scene: a trial due waits for a calm scene (900 presents at most), none begins where nothing is cut (a loading screen), and after a trial the scene moved under the next is never more than 900 presents late. The rule's "on" holds only for the scene it judged: the first window that moved from the first with the helper threads on takes them back off (`the rule's "on" withdrawn`), and the rule judges the new scene; and the rule's "on" needs three windows in a row, each within those margins of the first of them (an arrival whose texture load comes down a little each window passes two alike, not three) |
| `RX_VK_RECORDER=thread`, `=defer`, `=sync`, `=off` | (unset: `thread` on an NVIDIA GPU with 12 logical processors or more, and on an AMD GPU with 6 physical cores or more in the process's affinity but for AMD's Windows driver for RX 5000 and RX 6000 (`gpu/vk/recorder_auto.h`); else off. The log's `vk: recorder thread on/off by default` line says which rule applied; `RX_VK_LAB_AB=norec` makes the calls directly in its phases, to measure it) `thread`: the replay thread's `vkCmd*` calls packed with copies of their arguments into a ring and made by a recorder thread of their own, in order (`gpu/vk/vk_recorder.h`); the replay thread waits for it at every command buffer begin, end and reset, submit, wait, readback and destruction. Beside the Replay helper threads too (no longer refused with `RX_VK_PARALLEL`): a parallel replay's segments make their calls themselves, after the ring has been run (`segments_begin`), and the recorder is idle while they record. With it on, the frame statistics' `a frame's recorder thread` line says how many of the replay's calls it made a frame and in how long, and how long the replay thread waited for it; what it saves is the replay time line's `commands` with it against without it. `defer` and `sync` are its test steps (held and run on the same thread). `RX_VK_RECORDER_RING_MB` (8), `RX_VK_RECORDER_SPIN_US`, `RX_VK_RECORDER_STATS=0`, `RX_VK_RECORDER_POISON=1`, `RX_VK_RECORDER_PIN` (`next`, `sibling` or a mask) |
| `RX_VK_RECORDER_PARTS=0`, `=1`; `RX_VK_RECORDER_IDLE=wait` | (Triforce; unset: on with Threaded rendering (`RX_VK_THREADED`), so **off by default**) The recorder thread handed a part at a time (`gpu/vk/vk_recorder.h` `post_part`, `gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `part_submit`): the replay publishes a part's packets at its end (256 KB chunks of a long one) and puts the part's two `vkEndCommandBuffer` and its queue submit into the ring as one more packet, which the recorder makes in order, so the replay goes on into the next part at once; a slot's reuse waits for its own part, a fence or timeline wait for the parts handed over (at most its own time), a thread about to take the queue's lock for them first (`QueueMutex`, `gpu/vk/vk_internal.h`), and a holder of a queue lock never for a recorder waiting for that lock; the recorder waits on its ring between batches (WaitOnAddress). Not for a recording with upload pieces or with the lazy write-back (made on the replay's thread, as before). A `vk: recorder, by part` line with the stats line. `=1`: with any recorder thread; `=0`: as before. `RX_VK_RECORDER_IDLE=wait` (opt-in): the recorder waits on its ring after a 20 µs spin without the hand-off too |
| `RX_VK_REPLAY_PRIO=0` or `1` | (Triforce, on by default, after NX2navi's `recorder_prio`; `=0` as before) A thread runs at the highest priority while it replays the game's frames (the submitting thread's replay and its capture, the present, the stopped work the order thread lets on: `gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `ReplayPin`) and gets its own priority back after, so the game's code it runs between replays keeps the game's priority; the core kept for the replay is kept from the game's threads only, and the helper threads and the capture's copy helpers (above normal) outranked a replay at the game's priority there. A thread at that priority or above already is left alone. The replay thread of `RX_VK_ASYNC=1` and the recorder thread of `RX_VK_RECORDER=thread` take the level for good. `=1`: above normal. The log's `vk: a thread that replays runs at` line says which (`gpu/vk/replay_prio.h`) |
| `RX_VK_REPLAY_PIN_SOFT=0`, `=2` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's THREAD-09, after Source A's; Windows) A thread is pinned while it replays to the core the host keeps for the replay, and that core is kept from the game's threads, not from other programs': where another program's high-priority thread holds it, the pinned replay waits for it, ready to run (Source A, a 6+8-core hybrid processor with an overlay service at priority 15: 14 to 37 frames over 40 ms in a 40 s run, none with this). Not set: on a processor with cores of more than one kind the pin is soft: the thread may run on the reserved core and on the fastest-kind cores among its own processors, the reserved core's last logical processor is its ideal one, and the core it is on when the pin starts is left out so that it moves at once; its affinity and ideal processor come back after the replay. The replay thread of `RX_VK_ASYNC=1` gets the same processors for good. A processor of one kind of core keeps the strict pin, and nothing changes there. `0`: the reserved core alone everywhere, as before; `2`: soft everywhere (the lab; the knob `pinsoft` of `RX_VK_LAB_AB` does it by phases). A thread of another program that wakes on the core every millisecond moves the replay again and again (Source A: 54.3 against 44.6 fps, one run each): `0` is for that. The log's `vk: a replay's pin is` line says which (`gpu/vk/replay_pin.h`, `gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `ReplayPin`, `replay_pin_soft`) |
| `HOS_VCORES=0`, `HOS_CORES=<1..8>`, `HOS_RESERVE_CORE=0` | No virtual cores; the cores the game sees (6); no core kept for the replay (one is, with 8 or more logical processors) |
| `HOS_VCORES=0`, `HOS_CORES=<1..8>`, `HOS_RESERVE_CORE=0`, `=1` | No virtual cores; the cores the game sees (6); no core kept for the replay (one is, with 8 or more logical processors). (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's THREAD-49, after Source A's) On the cache die of a two-die X3D processor with SMT off none is kept unless `HOS_RESERVE_CORE=1` asks for it or the replay has a thread of its own (`RX_VK_ASYNC=1`): the game then has all 8 of the die's cores (Source A, a 9950X3D with SMT off: 59.8-59.9 fps with none kept against 56.9-57.4 with one). The log's `cpu: no core kept for the replay on the cache die` line says so. With SMT on and on every other processor nothing changes (`host/hos_x3d.h` `hos_x3d_keep_replay_core`) |
| `HOS_L3_HOME=0` | (Triforce, 11-23, Windows) On a processor with two or more L3 caches of the same size and 6+ cores under the first (a Ryzen 9 5900X, 5950X, 7900X, 7950X, 9900X, 9950X), the core kept for the replay is the first die's last core, not the processor's last on the second die, and the game's main and presenting threads are kept on the first die's other cores, so that what the main thread writes is in the L3 the replay reads it from; the game's other threads, the renderer's helpers and the driver's stay where Windows puts them. Nothing with one L3, with L3 caches of different sizes (`host/hos_x3d.h` has those), with fewer than 6 cores under the first, or with more than one processor group. The log's `cpu: logical processors ... share one of` line says what was found. `0`: as before (`host/hos_l3_home.h`, `host/hos_thread.c`, `tests/test_l3_home.py`) |
| `RX_VK_DYNRES_UNION=0` | (Triforce, 11-23) Resolution Auto's clock takes the frames' GPU time as the union of the recordings' timestamp intervals, each moment once (`gpu/vk/gpu_busy.h`). Their sum, as before with `0`, counted again the overlaps of AMD's first timestamps, written while the recording before still runs: 14.9 ms against a 7.8 ms span on an RX 6700 XT, and Auto drew the 3D at 854 x 480 on a card half idle. The union still counts a recording's waits on the GPU for events the CPU sets later (19.8 ms against a 5.9 ms span on the same card), so while the host does not wait for the GPU (its waits on all threads, `gpu_wait_kinds.h`, the compositor's image excluded: the card is the limit from 2 ms a frame on average, until they fall under 1 ms) the clock says at most the replay's span: only a card that holds the frame back steps the 3D down. `0` takes both away. The log's `vk: dynamic resolution's clock` line says which (`gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `ts_dyn_present`, `tests/test_gpu_busy.py`) |
| `RX_VK_ACQUIRE_AHEAD=0`, `=1` | (Triforce, 11-23, Windows) On AMD's driver the present thread (`RX_VK_PRESENT_THREAD`, wanted for this) acquires the next swapchain image right after its present and waits for it to be free there, while the presenting thread replays the frame; `present_image` then finds the image held and free and records its blit at once. An RX 6700 XT (26.11.2) waited 2.4 to 4.2 ms of every frame for the image in `present_image` (the pacing's wait in the present line), 16% of the presenting thread in a profile: AMD presents through DXGI and the image comes back with the compositor's refresh. Only with the acquire's fence (`RX_VK_ACQUIRE_FENCE`), without DLSS frame generation; nothing while an image is held or a resize or lost surface waits. A `vk: the next image acquired ahead` line every 600 presents. `0`: off; `1`: on any card (`gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `acquire_ahead`, `tests/test_acquire_ahead.py`) |
| `RX_VK_QUERY_RING=0` | (Triforce, 11-23) A recording's GPU timestamps are read from a mapped, host-coherent ring the GPU copies them into, not with `vkGetQueryPoolResults` (about 0.6 ms a frame on AMD's Windows driver on the replay thread, and a recorder boundary with `RX_VK_RECORDER=thread`): a slot's next use copies its last use's timestamps, each with its availability word, before it resets them, behind a transfer-to-host barrier; the slot's turn after that reads them, so the GPU time arrives a turn of the ring later (48 recordings). The upload command buffer's timestamps are read as before. Not without host-coherent memory. `0`: as before (`gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `query_ring_make`, `gpu_time_begin`, `gpu_time_collect`; `tests/test_query_ring.py`) |
| `HOS_AV1=mf`, `HOS_AV1_MISSING=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's IO-32; Windows) The game's AV1 movies on Media Foundation first (Microsoft's AV1 Video Extension), as before, and on dav1d only where the PC has no such decoder. Without it dav1d goes first where the FFmpeg the build links has libdav1d, as on Linux, and Media Foundation where it has not or where libdav1d does not open (`host/video_av1.h`, `host/nn_video.c` `dec_init_sized`): Media Foundation's path decodes in software on every GPU (`host/mf_av1.c` takes the synchronous decoder). `HOS_AV1=dav1d`, the old way to ask for dav1d first, is the default now. `HOS_AV1_MISSING=1`: Media Foundation as if the extension were missing. Each movie logs one line at its Stop, Flush or its decoder's end: its pictures, the decoder, the mean and the worst time to decode a submission, and the pictures skipped to catch up (`host/video_movie.h`). The two decoders were not timed against each other in the game |
| `HOS_VIDEO_RGBA=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's IO-36) Every planar 4:2:0 movie picture (libdav1d's) is converted to RGBA when it is decoded, as before: a swscale pass, an 8 MB allocation and an 8 MB row copy for a 1080p picture, on the movie thread. Without it such a picture is kept as NV12 alone, as Media Foundation's NV12 pictures already were, and its pool makes the RGBA only if the game reads RGBA, which this game's movie library does not; planar 4:2:0 with alpha is converted at once either way. The NV12 buffer is zeroed only for an odd size or a format that is not copied into it, and U and V are interleaved 16 pairs a step with SSE2 (`host/video_nv12.h`, `host/nn_video.c` `queue_frame`). The movie's line in the log (IO-32) has the time a picture took with either. Not timed in the game |
| `HOS_VIDEO_UNBOUNDED_CATCHUP=1`, `HOS_VIDEO_NO_CATCHUP=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's THREAD-82) The movie decoder's acquire skips the pictures behind the movie's clock and decodes the next ones to skip them too. `HOS_VIDEO_UNBOUNDED_CATCHUP=1`: with no bound but a guard of 600 steps, as before: with a decoder slower than the movie (19.5 ms a picture against 16.7) each picture decoded to catch up puts the clock further ahead, so an acquire decoded up to about 300 pictures with its lock held, threw them away, and the next started further behind. Without it each decode that gives a picture is timed, about the last 8 kept as one number, and the acquire stops decoding ahead when that number is more than the gap between two pictures (from the pictures' own times) or once it has decoded for one gap, and shows the late picture it has (`host/video_catchup.h`, `host/nn_video.c` `dec_acquire`): such a movie falls behind its sound instead of stalling, and its line in the log says "slower than it plays". A decoder faster than the movie catches up as before, a few pictures an acquire. `HOS_VIDEO_NO_CATCHUP=1`: no picture skipped at all (the movie in slow motion under 60 fps). Not run in the game |
| `HOS_VIDEO_NO_HOLD=1` | (Triforce) The movie decoder's acquire hands a picture out only when it is due: one more than 25 ms ahead of the movie's clock waits in the queue, and the buffer handed out shows the last picture again (`host/nn_video.c` `dec_acquire`; not a movie's first or last picture, and only for pools the game reads through `ReadDecoderOutputImage*`, as OoT's are). While a movie plays at the 60 cap the frames are paced at 240 and the game's count of the movie's 60ths follows the frames' time, not the clock, so the picture ran ahead of its sound (heard as the sound coming late). `HOS_VIDEO_NO_HOLD=1`: every picture handed out when asked, as before. The movie's end says in the log how often the last picture was shown again. `tests/test_video_hold_early.py`. Not run in the game |
| `HOS_VIDEO_INPUT_LIMIT=<n>` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's IO-34) How many submissions the movie decoder holds, not decoded yet or decoded and not taken, before it refuses one: 16 when unset; `0`: no limit, every submission taken, as before. The game submits a whole movie at once on the thread that also takes the pictures, so at a movie's start the pictures that came due while it submitted were skipped. With the limit a submission is refused at once with the console's result for a full decoder (0x1c266f) and the game submits it again later; the stream's last submission is never refused, and a decoder with no codec behind it refuses nothing. Each decoder has its own "ready for a submission" event, clear from a refusal until an acquire, a Stop or a Flush makes room (it was one event for all, always set); the movie's line in the log counts the refusals (`host/video_input.h`, `host/nn_video.c` `dec_submit`, `input_room`). If a movie ever hangs on a black screen, try `=0` first. Not run in the game |
| `HOS_MOVIE_PACE=0` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's PRESENT-30) The frame rate cap is kept while a movie plays, as before. Without it, at a cap of exactly 60 and with the Steady frame rate row off, the frames are paced by 240 while a movie player has updated in the last quarter second, and the game's own limiter and its time step follow as they do at a cap of 240 (`host/movie_pace.h`, `host/nn_vi.c` `movie_pace_decide`, `pace_fps`, `realtime_step`): the game's movie player takes one picture an update at most and its updates follow the frame loop, so at 60 every frame that came late was a picture the decoder skipped. Game time stays real time; the limiter is put back where it was when the movie is over, and the log has a line at the movie's start and one at its end. The cap itself (the Steady row, the vsync event, the hand-off to RivaTuner or Reflex) does not move. Movies use more CPU and power with it. Not run in the game |
| `A64_NO_VBYTES=1`, `A64_NO_VMOVES=1` | (Triforce, 11-21, the performance audit's LIFT-02; read when the game is translated, and in the lift key) The translator writes TBL with a one-register table and EXT as loops of single bytes through the stack, a 128-bit vector load as two halves and DUP by element as a loop, each followed by a 16-byte read of the register that cannot be forwarded from those stores. `nx2/fastvec.py` rewrites each on the lifted text as one instruction's helper (PSHUFB; PALIGNR or two shifts; one 16-byte load; one broadcast), the same bytes in the same registers (`tests/test_vbytes.py` runs every shape both ways). `A64_NO_VBYTES=1` keeps the translator's text for TBL and EXT, `A64_NO_VMOVES=1` for the loads and DUP. A build with them needs a hot profile of its own (`RX_PGO=gen`) |
| `A64_DMB_LIGHT=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's LIFT-47, after Source B's; read when the game is translated, and in the lift key; off by default) The translator writes every DMB and DSB as `recomp_barrier()`, an out-of-line full fence. x86-64 keeps load-load, load-store and store-store order in hardware, so with this set the eight DMBs that ask only for those (ISHLD, ISHST and the OSH, NSH and full-system forms) are lifted as `A64_DMB_LDST()` (`nx2/insn.py` `dmb_light`), which is nothing but a barrier for the compiler on x86-64 and the full fence on any other host (`nx2/rtgen.py`). A full DMB, every DSB, ISB and STLR's locked store keep theirs. Unset, empty or `0`: the kit's text. `tests/test_dmb_light.py` runs every such word both ways, alone and between a store and a load. Not counted in this game's code and not measured in the game: how many of its barriers are of these kinds is not known, and what other threads see cannot be shown by a test on one thread. With it set the hot functions that hold such a DMB no longer match the shipped hot profile: such a build needs one of its own (`RX_PGO=gen`) |
| `HOS_FMA_FAST=0`, `A64_NO_FMA_SOFT=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's LIFT-06, after Source A's; only in a build without FMA3, x86-64-v2: with FMA3 none of it is compiled) Without a hardware FMA every double-precision FMADD, and each single-precision one its binary64 fast path cannot prove, ran the translator's exact body, integer code of many words (about 180 ns a call and 13.7% of a core on an i7-3770, Source A's figures). `nx2/fma_soft.py` puts a short way out at the head of `recomp_fpe_fma64` and `recomp_fpe_fma32` for the plain case: default FPCR (FZ or not), three normal operands and a normal result, by an exact product and sum in binary64, rounded to odd, then rounded once, FPSR.IXC set when inexact; everything else goes on into the body. `tests/test_fma_soft.py` compares both bodies with it and without, value and every byte of the context. `HOS_FMA_FAST=0` (read once when the game runs): the exact bodies only, as before. `A64_NO_FMA_SOFT=1` (read when the game is translated, and in the lift key): the translator's runtime text as it was. Not measured in a game on a CPU without FMA3 |
| `A64_NO_FMA_STEPS=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's LIFT-05, after Source A's; read when the game is translated, and in the lift key; only in a build with FMA3, x86-64-v3) The single-precision FRECPS and FRSQRTS steps (2 - a*b and (3 - a*b) / 2, the Newton-Raphson steps of a reciprocal and a reciprocal square root) were computed in binary64 on x86-64 (two widenings, a multiply, a subtraction, a narrowing) with a test for a binary32 midpoint, which went to the exact body. `nx2/fma_steps.py` gives their fast paths the guard the fused multiply-add's already had: with FMA3 one fused instruction, the step rounded once as on the console; the tests that decide whether the result is kept are unchanged (`tests/test_fma_steps.py` runs every form both ways and against the exact body). `A64_NO_FMA_STEPS=1`: the translator's text for the two steps. The compile define `-DA64_FPX_NO_FMA32` returns all three (FMADD/FMLA and the two steps) to the binary64 form. Source A measured the steps alone at 23% to 29% fewer cycles; not measured in the game, and how often this game runs them is not counted |
| `A64_VECFP=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's LIFT-03, after Source A's; read when the game is translated, and in the lift key; off by default) The translator's passes turn straight FADD, FSUB and FMUL .4S and the .4S by-element multiplies into one SSE op; other float forms stay a helper call a lane or exact integer code a lane. With this set `nx2/vecfp.py` lifts each of them as `if (!a64_vfpx_q_<form>(c, ...)) { the C as before }`: the helper does the op in SSE and writes the register only when the scalar fast paths' rules prove the result is the console's with no new FPSR bit. `1`: every group; or a list of `elem` (FMUL, FMLA, FMLS .2S by element), `vec` (FMUL .2S; FMLA, FMLS and FDIV .2S and .4S; the multiply-adds need FMA3 and decline without it), `minmax` (FMAX, FMIN, FMAXNM, FMINNM: scalar S, .2S, .4S; kept unless a NaN or, under FZ, a subnormal is involved) and `fcvt` (FCVT S to D and D to S, FCVTL, FCVTL2, FCVTN). Unset, empty or `0`: the translator's text. `tests/test_vecfp.py` runs 137 guest words three ways (with it, without it, exact). Source A measured +3.5% to +5.1% fps where the game's update bounds the frame, for 3.7% more compile time; not measured in this game, which is why it is off. A build with it needs a hot profile of its own (`RX_PGO=gen`) |
| `A64_SUB_V=1`, `A64_SUB_V=ovf` | (Triforce; read when the game is translated, and in the lift key; off by default) The translator writes a flag-setting subtraction's V flag (SUBS, CMP, CCMP) from the bits of its operands and result, and the game's signed conditions (LT, GE, GT, LE) read it against N: seven to nine host instructions a compare, which the compiler does not fold. `1` writes V as N xor (a < b, signed), the same bit for every operand, which the compiler folds to one host compare and jump. It adds a `<` to every function that compares, and the hot pieces' recorded profile is matched to a function by a count of such things: record a new profile with it. `ovf` writes V as the compiler's own overflow test of the subtraction (`__builtin_sub_overflow`): five instructions a signed compare, and the recorded profile still matches. `nx2/cmpflags.py`; `tests/test_cmpflags.py` runs both forms against the translator's text. |
| `RX_MARK_BITMAP=1` (build), `HOS_MARK_BITMAP_ALL=1` (run) | (Triforce; read by `nx2/build.py`: a full rebuild; off by default) Every store of the translated game also marks its memory page, so that the renderer finds what the game changed; the renderer reads only the marks of the pages the GPU can reach, and most stores are to other memory. With `RX_MARK_BITMAP=1` a store marks only when its first byte is on a page whose bit is set in a bitmap (`host/mark_bitmap.c`): the pages of every GPU mapping and native pool, and the page before each, set when the mapping is made and never cleared. A mark missed is stale data drawn, so compare pictures before shipping it. In such a build `HOS_MARK_BITMAP_ALL=1` sets every bit at the start, so every store marks as before (the control of an A/B). `tests/test_mark_bitmap.py`. |
| `A64_FASTFP=1`, `=hot`, `=check`; `A64_FASTFP_MIN=<n>`, `A64_FASTFP_STORES=0`, `A64_FASTFP_VMOVES=0` (lift); `HOS_NO_FASTFP=1` (run) | (Triforce; read when the game is translated, and in the lift key; off by default) Fast float regions (`nx2/fastfp.py`). Each single-precision float op of the translated game carries its own exactness tests, about 25 instructions around one SSE op. With `A64_FASTFP=1` a straight run of instructions holding at least `A64_FASTFP_MIN` (8) such ops is written twice: a fast copy of plain SSE ops between one MXCSR clear and one read, and the exact copy as before, which runs instead, with the run's registers put back, when the fast copy raised an invalid, denormal or underflow flag. A run may hold guest stores (made after the check passed; `A64_FASTFP_STORES=0`: a store ends a run) and the vector moves of `nx2/fastvec.py` (`A64_FASTFP_VMOVES=0`: they end a run). `hot`: only the hot functions. `check`: both copies every time, the exact one's result kept and the two compared (`fastfp check:` lines in the log). A function that holds an address the game's patches name gets no region, and the lift stops if a patch changes a function that has one. In a build lifted with it, `HOS_NO_FASTFP=1` runs the exact copies only. It changes the text of every function with a region (3,651 of them, 268 of the 849 hot ones in the supported game): record a new profile for the hot pieces with it. `tests/test_fastfp_override.py`, `test_fastfp_regions.py`, `test_fastfp_fma.py`. |
| `HOS_HEAP_COMMIT=1` | (Triforce, 11-21, the performance audit's MEM-09) On Windows the game's heap (~6.9 GB asked for at its start) is left reserved and committed as it is touched, 64 KB at a first touch, by the handler that commits pages nothing handed out; committed whole, all of it counted against RAM plus the page file, touched or not. A fault another thread's commit answered is tried again, a commit refused at Windows' commit limit is tried again for 5 s, and a file read or write reads each page of its buffer once before the kernel's call, which faults nothing in. The log says `heap: left reserved` and counts the heap's first touches. `1`: all of it committed at the start, as before; so it is in large pages (`HOS_LARGE_PAGES`) and on Linux (`host/heap_commit.h`, `mkw_glue.c` `commit_on_touch`, `hos_mem.c` `SetMemoryHeapSize`) |
| `RX_VK_DMABUF_NVIDIA=1`, `RX_VK_NO_DMABUF=1`, `RX_VK_DMABUF_PIECE_MB=<n>` | (Linux) Where the game's memory is a memfd, each pool of it is offered to the driver as a dma-buf made by udmabuf, larger pools as several of at most udmabuf's limit bound into one sparse buffer; the log's `vk: guest memory as dma-bufs:` line says yes or why not. `RX_VK_NO_DMABUF=1`: host-pointer imports only. `RX_VK_DMABUF_PIECE_MB=<n>`: pieces of at most n MB. (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's OS-08, after Source A's; not yet run on Linux) NVIDIA's proprietary driver refuses every such dma-buf and the pool ends as a host-pointer import anyway, after a refused import each (up to 198 ms for one during a load) and a kernel message at each start: with that driver none is offered, `/dev/udmabuf` is not opened, and the line says `no (NVIDIA's driver refuses them: host pointers)` (`gpu/vk/dmabuf_gate.h`). NVK and RADV keep their dma-bufs. `RX_VK_DMABUF_NVIDIA=1`: offered to NVIDIA's driver too, as before |
| `HOS_COMMIT_WAIT_MS=<ms>` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's OS-04, after Source A's and Source B's; 5000 unless set) How long a refusal at Windows' commit limit (RAM and the page file full, error 1455) is waited out, tried again every 50 ms: such a refusal often ends in moments, as a page file set to grow grows or commit is given back. The game's own commits waited so already (the row above); now the host's own tables do too (a failed allocation there ended the game at once: `host/host_alloc.h`, `hos_xmalloc` in `host/mkw_glue.c`), and the renderer's failed `new`, which was an uncaught `std::bad_alloc`: its new handler sleeps 50 ms and lets the allocation be tried again, up to this long a thread, but only while the system has less than 4 GiB of commit left (a `new` refused with more free is not the limit's and throws at once), and not for the few allocations that have their own answer to a refusal (`gpu/vk/new_wait.h`, `gpu/vk/vk_main.cpp` `new_refused`). The log has `refused ... made after N ms` for the first rescues. `0`: no wait anywhere, a refusal fails at once; 60000 at most |
| `HOS_WS_FLOOR_MB=<MB>`, `=0` | (Triforce, 11-24; Windows only) At the game's start the process asks Windows for a soft working-set minimum, a quarter of the RAM and at most 4096 MB: when the PC runs short of memory Windows trims the other processes, and the game's pages above the minimum, before it trims the game below it (left at Windows' own minimum of a few hundred KB, a game with other programs open was trimmed to about 1 GB resident, and its frames waited on its threads' page faults). Soft: Windows still trims below it when memory is critically short. `<MB>`: that minimum instead, at most half the RAM; `0` (or `off`, `no`, `false`): Windows' own, as before. The log has `mem: the working set's minimum is ...` (`host/ws_floor.h`, `host/hos_main.c` `ws_floor`) |
| `HOS_TEXELS_KEEP_CORE=1` | (Triforce, 11-21, the performance audit's THREAD-20) `nvnTextureReadTexels` and `nvnTextureWriteTexels` reach the back end, which waits for all the GPU work submitted and then for the replay's lock; the game calls them from its loading threads at an area's arrival and at boot. The calling thread gives its virtual core up for that call, as it does around a readback, so the game threads allowed only on that core do not wait with it. A write kept for a renderer not started yet never waits and keeps the core. `1`: kept, as before (`host/rx_backend.c` `rx_nvn_read_texels`, `rx_nvn_write_texels`) |
| `HOS_LOG_SYNC=1`, `HOS_LOG_BATCH_MS=<ms>` | The log's lines are written by a thread of their own, not by the thread that logs: a write that blocks (a console, a busy disk) holds no game thread. The writer waits a little after a line and writes the lines of that time in one write (`HOS_LOG_BATCH_MS`, 250 unless set, at most 2000; 0: the lines there are at each wake); a queue half full goes out at once, and every way out of the game writes the queue first. `HOS_LOG_SYNC=1`: each line written by the thread that logs it. (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's THREAD-56, after Source A's; not yet run on Linux) The writer is Linux's and the Steam Deck's too now: there every `hos_log` wrote stderr in the calling game thread, its virtual core held, so `HOS_LOG_SYNC=1` is Linux as it was. And `nvnCommandBufferEndRecording`'s two lines (the sweep's, the 50,000-recording line) are written after the recordings' lock is released, the virtual core given up, on every system (`host/hos_thread.c` `log_queue`, `host/nvn_native.c`) |
| `RX_VK_INVALIDATE_QUICK=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's THREAD-25, after Source A's; off unless set. Source A has it on; here it waits for a run in the game, as a wrong answer skips the wait a readback of GPU results needs, the exposure flashes' kind of fault) The game invalidates each shape's 64-byte occlusion slot, per view, and the host gives the calling thread's virtual core up before every such call and queues for it again after, though nearly none has anything to wait for. With `1` the back end is asked first, with no lock and no wait: a range with nothing to invalidate, or one below 512 GB with no fast fence poll's order ticket still to wait for, no live texture's storage in it, no texture the GPU wrote ever listed there and no write-back ever made into its pages, is one `invalidate` returns from at once, so the call is left out and the core kept. The two "ever" answers are bits a page that are only set, before their tables change (`gpu/vk/page_marks.h`); a page written back once has the call made for good. Source A measured Hyrule Field at 50.5 to 51.4 fps and its 1% low up by two to four frames a second, its busiest worker keeping the core 31,444 times against 2,604. Every 10 s a `vk: invalidates:` line counts the calls left out and made. With `RX_VK_LOG_INVALIDATE`, `RX_VK_LOG_READBACK` or `RX_VK_INVALIDATE_WAIT` set every call is made; the lab's knob `noinvq` makes them all in its phases (`gpu/vk/vk_replay.cpp` `invalidate_quick`, `host/rx_backend.c` `rx_nvn_invalidate`) |
| `HOS_WRITE_KEEP_CORE=1` | (Triforce, 11-24) nn::fs::WriteFile and FlushFile keep their virtual core through the host's call, as before. The default gives it up, as the reads and the open do: a slow write or flush (a busy or network disk, a virus scanner) held every game thread pinned to the writing thread's core for as long (`host/nn_fs.c`) |
| `HOS_OCB_HOLD_MS=<ms>` | (Triforce, 11-24; 2000 unless set) The neighbouring map the game's OptimizeChangeBank thread preloads is kept while the game's choice only flickers. The game picks one neighbour each frame from Link's position and heading, and another choice cancels the one loading or loaded and loads the other from the start: standing or circling between two neighbours (Castle Town's market, between Hyrule Field's and the castle's) reloaded them over and over, the loader threads busy the whole time. A choice that names another map than the one held is taken only once it has stayed the choice that many milliseconds without a break; a first choice, a direct request and the game's other mode are the game's own. `0`, a negative number or `off`: the game's own choice at every frame, as before. A preload can start up to that long later when Link turns towards another neighbour and keeps going. The supported game build only, and only while the manager is laid out as `host/oot_ocb_hold.h` knows it; the log's `ocb:` lines say the hold and, every 18,000 updates, the choices held back and taken (`host/oot_ocb_hold.c`, `nx2/oot_patches.py` `patch_ocb_hold`, `tests/test_ocb_hold.py`) |
| `HOS_OOT_BARRIER_WAIT=0` | (Triforce, 11-24; on by default, not measured here) The game's job barriers wait for their work (`host/oot_barrier.h`, `host/oot_barrier.c`, `nx2/oot_patches.py` `patch_barrier_wait`, `tests/test_oot_barrier.py`). Three of its parallel-for jobs (f_800998a0, f_800738e0, f_8007e200) are run by several threads over one job block, each taking items by atomic ORs into bitmasks; a thread with nothing left to take slept 20 us and looked again until the masks it polls were full. A sleep that short wakes ~0.5 ms late on Windows with 6 or fewer logical processors (`HOS_SHORT_SLEEP_TIMER` below), and above 6 it is yielded away on a processor. Now the thread waits (its virtual core given up, as for a sleep) until a polled mask word moves, woken right after the OR that moves it, at most 1 ms, and then looks again as after its sleep: a wake that never came costs that millisecond, never the job. Source A's in-run A/Bs in Castle Town's market: frame time -3.2 % on 4 cores, no difference on 16. The log counts the waits every 16,384 (`barrier:` lines: moved already, woken by a move, the whole 1 ms). `0`, `off`, `no`, `false`: the sleeps, as before. The supported game build only; a lift where a function's code differs keeps that function's sleeps (the lift's warning says so) |
| `HOS_STEAM_PADS=0` | (Triforce, 11-24; on unless set to `0`, `off`, `no` or `false`) Valve's controllers under Steam Input (`host/steam_pads.h`, `host/pad_sdl.c`, `tests/test_steam_pads.py`). Three rules, none of which a player without Steam Input or a Steam Deck meets. Started from Steam (its `SDL_GAMECONTROLLER_IGNORE_DEVICES` list and its `SDL_GAMECONTROLLER_ALLOW_STEAM_VIRTUAL_GAMEPAD` both in the environment), Valve's own pads but the Steam Deck's come out of that list, so SDL opens the real pad beside Steam's stand-in and Motion controls can read its gyroscope; only with an SDL that tells a stand-in pad apart (SDL 2.30 or SDL3 3.2 on). While a stand-in pad (one with a Steam Input handle) is open, a real Valve pad gives its motion only: its buttons, sticks and rumble go through the stand-in, once. And while the Steam Deck's built-in controls are open as a pad of their own (USB 28de:1205, no Steam handle: Desktop Mode, the game started outside Steam), the mouse steers nothing and its buttons are no control's (`host/nn_hid.c` `read_input`), because Steam's Desktop layout presses that mouse's buttons for the triggers and R2 was Y as well; the game's menus keep the pointer. `0`: Steam's list as it is, every open pad's buttons counted, the mouse as before. The log's `hid:` lines say each rule as it applies |
| `HOS_VSYNC_TIMER=1` | (Triforce, 11-24; off unless set) The vsync ticker (`host/nn_vi.c` `vsync_main`, the thread that signals the display's vsync event at the frame rate cap) waits on a high-resolution timer for every wait. Not set, a wait under a millisecond yields until its time, as the host's other short sleeps do, so with a cap of 1000 the ticker keeps a processor busy. The timer wakes about half a millisecond late, and the game's frame limiter waits for this event: not yet run in the game, so what it does to the frames' pacing above a few hundred frames a second is not known |
| `HOS_LOG_REDACT=0` | (Triforce, 11-24) The log's lines are written as they are. The default leaves the player's user name out of what the game saves, as players pass `triforce_log.txt` around: in each line the game and the renderer log, and in a fatal error's, the home folder reads `%USERPROFILE%` (`~` off Windows; its short 8.3 name too) and the user's name, where it is a folder of a path (another drive's `Users` folder, `/run/media`), reads `<user>`; the rest of each path stays. A bug capture's `log-tail.txt` is redacted as a whole, the lines other code wrote to stderr itself too (the Vulkan loader, SDL); in `triforce_log.txt` itself those lines are redacted once the run has ended: by the crash window's helper as soon as the game is gone, else by the next start on the log it rotates to `triforce_log.1.txt` (read whole once at that start; up to 256 MB). With `=0` the file is not read either. The crash window's Report copy is always redacted (`host/log_redact.c`, `host/redact_line.h`, `tests/test_log_redact.py`) |
| `HOS_CRASH_WATCH=0` | (Triforce, 11-24; on unless set to `0`, `off`, `false` or `no`) No window after a crash. By default the game starts a small windowless helper of itself (`Triforce --crash-watch`, `host/hos_crash_dialog.c`) right after its log's `run_id:` line; it waits for the game to end and, after a crash, a start that could not go on, or an end with no clean exit the player did not ask for, shows "Triforce closed unexpectedly" (or "couldn't start") with why where the record or the log says, and Report (the error, the PC, the end of the log, home folder and user name out: copied to the clipboard and saved in `crash-reports/pending`; its box opens that folder) and Open the log folder. Nothing is sent and no web page opens. None for `--prepare-shaders`, `HOS_HEADLESS`, `HOS_WINDOW_NOACTIVATE`, `HOS_PGO` or under gamescope; quiet after the game's own "could not start its renderer" box. The records themselves (`crash-reports/running`, `pending`: `host/hos_crash_record.c`) are kept either way, beside the crash report in `triforce_log.txt`, which is unchanged (`tests/test_crash_record.py`, `tests/test_crash_dialog.py`) |
| `HOS_CRASH_MINIDUMP=1` | (Triforce, 11-24; off unless set) After a crash's record, a private Windows minidump in `crash-reports/local-only` (no data segments, no extra memory), with an outcome note; never part of a report (`host/hos_crash_record.c`) |
| `HOS_CRASH_TEST=start`, `=<seconds>` | (Triforce, 11-24; from the environment only, not `triforce_env.txt`) The game crashes on purpose, just after its crash handlers are set up or that many seconds later, to see the crash record and the window (`host/hos_crash_dialog.c`) |
| `HOS_OPEN_KEEP_CORE=1` | (Triforce, 11-21, the performance audit's IO-24) nn::fs::OpenFile gives its virtual core up only around the host's own open, as before. The default gives it up before the path is worked out too: that reaches the button prompts' builder, which builds a file at its first open (a quarter of a second and more) while the other openers wait for it, and none of them holds a core meanwhile. Every return takes the core back before it answers the game (`host/nn_fs.c` OpenFile, `host/prompts.c` `hos_prompts_font_file`) |
| `HOS_SHORT_SLEEP_TIMER=1`, `=0` | (Triforce, 11-21, the performance audit's OS-31) How sleeps under a millisecond wait. Not set: with 6 or fewer logical processors in the process's mask (Windows, one processor group) the game's own short sleeps wait on the high-resolution timer, which wakes about 0.5 ms late, and the host's own yield until their time; with more, all yield, as before. The game's idle job workers sleep 20 us and look again about 7,600 times a second, and a yielding sleeper keeps a processor busy. `1` (any value but 0, off, no, false): every short sleep on the timer, as the switch did before; `0`: every short sleep yields. (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's OS-32, after Source A's; not yet run on Linux) Off Windows the processors are not counted and, not set, every short sleep yields, as before; `HOS_SHORT_SLEEP_TIMER=game` puts the game's own short sleeps on the timer and leaves the host's yielding at any processor count, on any system: Source A's default off Windows (on Linux the timer wakes tens of microseconds late, not half a millisecond), which was never measured in the game by itself and is to be tried on a Steam Deck before `SHORT_SLEEP_ELSEWHERE` in `host/short_sleep.h` makes it what Linux does when the switch is not set. The log's `sleep:` line says which (`host/short_sleep.h`, `host/hos_thread.c` `sleep_for`) |
| `HOS_DISCARD_KEEP_CORE=0`, `=1` | (Triforce, 11-22, the performance audit's OS-40, after Source A's; not set: as before, which is NOT Source A's default.
